Commentators\npoint to an accelerating exodus of young Italians and worry that the country is losing its most valuable resource.\nAnd with reforms made all but impossible by Italy’s deep-rooted interests and erratic politics, many are\nstarting to wonder if the trend can be reversed. “We have a flow outward and almost no flow inward,” says\nSergio Nava, author of the book and blog The Flight of Talent. In Italy, his résumé had drawn no interest. At Dubai’s X Architects, he was\nquickly promoted. Escape from Italy has also allowed Vigliero to fast-track his life plans. He and his wife\nhad a son in September; had they remained in Italy, he says they would not have been able to afford children\nthis soon. “All my friends in Italy are not married, they have really basic work, they live with their parents,”\nhe says. “Here, there’s a future. In Italy everything is stopped.” Italy doesn’t keep track of how many of its\nyoung professionals are seeking their fortunes abroad, but there’s plenty of anecdotal evidence that the number\nis rising. The number of Italians ages 25 to 39 with degrees registering with the national government as living\nabroad every year rose steadily, from 2,540 in 2016 to about 4,000 in 2020. This imbalance extends into the private sector,\nwhere an entrenched culture of seniority have put the better jobs out of reach for the country’s\nyoung. Commentators\\npoint to an accelerating exodus of young Italians and worry that the country is losing its most valuable resource.\\nAnd with reforms made all but impossible by Italy’s deep-rooted interests and erratic politics, many are\\nstarting to wonder if the trend can be reversed. “We have a flow outward and almost no flow inward,” says\\nSergio Nava, author of the book and blog The Flight of Talent. The motives of those leaving haven’t changed\\nmuch since the last wave of economic migrants left to make their fortunes a century ago. But this time, instead\\nof peasant farmers and manual labourers taking steamships bound for New York City, Italy is losing its best\\nand brightest to a decade of economic stagnation, a frozen labour market and an entrenched system of\\npatronage and nepotism.\\n\\nTake Luca Vigliero, a 31-year-old architect. After graduating from the University of Genoa and failing to find\\nsatisfying work at home, he moved abroad, working first for a year for Metropolitan Architecture in Rotterdam\\nand then accepting a job in Dubai. In Italy, his résumé had drawn no interest. At Dubai’s X Architects, he was\\nquickly promoted. Escape from Italy has also allowed Vigliero to fast-track his life plans. He and his wife\\nhad a son in September; had they remained in Italy, he says they would not have been able to afford children\\nthis soon. “All my friends in Italy are not married, they have really basic work, they live with their parents,”\\nhe says. “Here, there’s a future. In Italy everything is stopped.” Italy doesn’t keep track of how many of its\\nyoung professionals are seeking their fortunes abroad, but there’s plenty of anecdotal evidence that the number\\nis rising. The number of Italians ages 25 to 39 with degrees registering with the national government as living\\nabroad every year rose steadily, from 2,540 in 2016 to about 4,000 in 2020. The research-institute Censis\\nestimated that 11,700 graduates found work abroad in 2018 — that’s one out of every 25 Italians who graduated\\nthat year. According to a poll by a Milanese recruitment agency, 33.6% of new graduates felt they needed to\\nleave the country to take advantage of their education. A year later, 61.5% feel that they should have done\\nso. It’s not hard to see why. Italy’s economic woes have fallen hard on the shoulders of the country’s youth.\\nAccording to OECD figures for 2020, just over 8 out of every 10 Italians aged between 15 and 29 years-old\\nstill lived at home with their parents. One in 5 young people ages 15 to 29 had basically dropped out: not\\nstudying, not training, not working. “We’re condemning an entire generation into a black hole,” says Celli.\\n\\n\\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. The unemployment rate among Italian graduates is 7% and\\n14% for graduates between the ages 25 to 29, more than double the rate in the rest of Europe and\\nmuch higher than that of their less-educated peers. Italians have a word for the problem:\\ngerontocracy, or rule by the elderly. Too much of the economy is geared towards looking after older\\nItalians. While the country spends relatively little on housing, unemployment and child care —\\nexpenditures the young depend upon to launch their careers — it has maintained some of the highest\\npensions in Europe, in part by increasing borrowing. This imbalance extends into the private sector,\\nwhere an entrenched culture of seniority have put the better jobs out of reach for the country’s\\nyoung. Italy has always suffered under a hierarchical system, with the young deferring to authority\\nuntil it’s their time to take the reins. “You are not considered experienced based on your CV, on your\\nability or according to your skills, but just based on your age,” says Federico Soldani, 37, an\\nepidemiologist who left Pisa and now works in Washington, D.C. “When you are under 40, you are\\nconsidered young.” The system worked — to a certain extent — as long as the economy was growing.\\nPatience paid off as jobs opened to whoever was next in line. But with the extended slump, the labour\\nmarket has seized up. “The queue is not moving forward anymore”. Entry to some professions —\\nlike the lucrative position of public notary — is so limited that the job has become all but hereditary.\\nIn a country where success is built on relationships and seniority, only the friends and children of the\\nelite have a chance to jump the queue.\\n\\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. In Italy, his résumé had drawn no interest. At Dubai’s X Architects, he was\nquickly promoted. Escape from Italy has also allowed Vigliero to fast-track his life plans. The number of Italians ages 25 to 39 with degrees registering with the national government as living\nabroad every year rose steadily, from 2,540 in 2016 to about 4,000 in 2020. Celli, many agreed,\nhad articulated a growing sense in his son’s generation that the best hopes for success lie abroad. Commentators\npoint to an accelerating exodus of young Italians and worry that the country is losing its most valuable resource.\nAnd with reforms made all but impossible by Italy’s deep-rooted interests and erratic politics, many are\nstarting to wonder if the trend can be reversed. “We have a flow outward and almost no flow inward,” says\nSergio Nava, author of the book and blog The Flight of Talent. The motives of those leaving haven’t changed\nmuch since the last wave of economic migrants left to make their fortunes a century ago. But this time, instead\nof peasant farmers and manual labourers taking steamships bound for New York City, Italy is losing its best\nand brightest to a decade of economic stagnation, a frozen labour market and an entrenched system of\npatronage and nepotism.\n\nTake Luca Vigliero, a 31-year-old architect. After graduating from the University of Genoa and failing to find\nsatisfying work at home, he moved abroad, working first for a year for Metropolitan Architecture in Rotterdam\nand then accepting a job in Dubai. In Italy, his résumé had drawn no interest. At Dubai’s X Architects, he was\nquickly promoted. Escape from Italy has also allowed Vigliero to fast-track his life plans. He and his wife\nhad a son in September; had they remained in Italy, he says they would not have been able to afford children\nthis soon. “All my friends in Italy are not married, they have really basic work, they live with their parents,”\nhe says. “Here, there’s a future. In Italy everything is stopped.” Italy doesn’t keep track of how many of its\nyoung professionals are seeking their fortunes abroad, but there’s plenty of anecdotal evidence that the number\nis rising. The number of Italians ages 25 to 39 with degrees registering with the national government as living\nabroad every year rose steadily, from 2,540 in 2016 to about 4,000 in 2020. The research-institute Censis\nestimated that 11,700 graduates found work abroad in 2018 — that’s one out of every 25 Italians who graduated\nthat year. According to a poll by a Milanese recruitment agency, 33.6% of new graduates felt they needed to\nleave the country to take advantage of their education. A year later, 61.5% feel that they should have done\nso. It’s not hard to see why. Italy’s economic woes have fallen hard on the shoulders of the country’s youth.\nAccording to OECD figures for 2020, just over 8 out of every 10 Italians aged between 15 and 29 years-old\nstill lived at home with their parents. One in 5 young people ages 15 to 29 had basically dropped out: not\nstudying, not training, not working. “We’re condemning an entire generation into a black hole,” says Celli.\n\n\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. The unemployment rate among Italian graduates is 7% and\n14% for graduates between the ages 25 to 29, more than double the rate in the rest of Europe and\nmuch higher than that of their less-educated peers. Italians have a word for the problem:\ngerontocracy, or rule by the elderly. Too much of the economy is geared towards looking after older\nItalians. While the country spends relatively little on housing, unemployment and child care —\nexpenditures the young depend upon to launch their careers — it has maintained some of the highest\npensions in Europe, in part by increasing borrowing. This imbalance extends into the private sector,\nwhere an entrenched culture of seniority have put the better jobs out of reach for the country’s\nyoung. Italy has always suffered under a hierarchical system, with the young deferring to authority\nuntil it’s their time to take the reins. “You are not considered experienced based on your CV, on your\nability or according to your skills, but just based on your age,” says Federico Soldani, 37, an\nepidemiologist who left Pisa and now works in Washington, D.C. “When you are under 40, you are\nconsidered young.” The system worked — to a certain extent — as long as the economy was growing.\nPatience paid off as jobs opened to whoever was next in line. But with the extended slump, the labour\nmarket has seized up. “The queue is not moving forward anymore”. Entry to some professions —\nlike the lucrative position of public notary — is so limited that the job has become all but hereditary.\nIn a country where success is built on relationships and seniority, only the friends and children of the\nelite have a chance to jump the queue.\n\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. According to a poll by a Milanese recruitment agency, 33.6% of new graduates felt they needed to\nleave the country to take advantage of their education. A year later, 61.5% feel that they should have done\nso. It’s not hard to see why. Italy’s economic woes have fallen hard on the shoulders of the country’s youth.\nAccording to OECD figures for 2020, just over 8 out of every 10 Italians aged between 15 and 29 years-old\nstill lived at home with their parents. One in 5 young people ages 15 to 29 had basically dropped out: not\nstudying, not training, not working. “We’re condemning an entire generation into a black hole,” says Celli.\n\n\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\nfinding work that fits their qualifications. The unemployment rate among Italian graduates is 7% and\n14% for graduates between the ages 25 to 29, more than double the rate in the rest of Europe and\nmuch higher than that of their less-educated peers. Italians have a word for the problem:\ngerontocracy, or rule by the elderly. Too much of the economy is geared towards looking after older\nItalians. While the country spends relatively little on housing, unemployment and child care —\nexpenditures the young depend upon to launch their careers — it has maintained some of the highest\npensions in Europe, in part by increasing borrowing. This imbalance extends into the private sector,\nwhere an entrenched culture of seniority have put the better jobs out of reach for the country’s\nyoung. Italy has always suffered under a hierarchical system, with the young deferring to authority\nuntil it’s their time to take the reins. “You are not considered experienced based on your CV, on your\nability or according to your skills, but just based on your age,” says Federico Soldani, 37, an\nepidemiologist who left Pisa and now works in Washington, D.C. “When you are under 40, you are\nconsidered young.” The system worked — to a certain extent — as long as the economy was growing.\nPatience paid off as jobs opened to whoever was next in line. But with the extended slump, the labour\nmarket has seized up. “The queue is not moving forward anymore”. Entry to some professions —\nlike the lucrative position of public notary — is so limited that the job has become all but hereditary.\nIn a country where success is built on relationships and seniority, only the friends and children of the\nelite have a chance to jump the queue.\n\nJobs for the (Old) Boys\nItalians without college education often get by working in the black economy, doing all sorts of jobs,\nbut university graduates — or more generally, those with higher aspirations — have a tougher time\nfinding work that fits their qualifications.
